Reading the Analects Through Self-as-an-End · Part II

Part II · The Man Who Would Not Be a Sage
解论语 · 矿脉

Han Qin (秦汉) · Independent Researcher · 2026
DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.22656223 · Full PDF on Zenodo · CC BY 4.0

What he said about himself, and what was seen of him

What this Part is doing

Volume Two built the instrument. This one takes it into the richest seam in the book: what Confucius said about himself, and what his students recorded of him when he was not making a point.

Six books, of very different character. Books 5 and 6 sort people — the same word, ren, withheld from one student after another. Book 7 is the densest concentration of self-report in the text, and it is the heart of this Part. Book 8 is the first place where material of clearly different provenance sits side by side. Book 9 gives the method and the one first-hand account from a student's side. And Book 10 is the sparsest book in the entire text — which turns out to be its most useful lesson.

One theme runs through: everything here removes authority from him. That is the pattern that is hard to explain any other way.

1. The systematic removal of his own standing

Book 7 contains a sequence that a school building a sage would not have produced.

He declines innate knowledge:

"I am not one who was born knowing. I love what is old, and I am quick to seek it." （我非生而知之者，好古，敏以求之者也。）— 7.19

He reports himself unfinished:

"As for acting the part of the noble person — that I have not yet managed." （躬行君子，则吾未之有得。）— 7.32

He refuses both of the highest titles:

"Sage, and ren — how would I dare? What can be said is that I work at it without tiring and teach without weariness — that is all." （若圣与仁，则吾岂敢？抑为之不厌，诲人不倦，则可谓云尔已矣。）— 7.33

He says he has never met the type:

"A sage I have not managed to see." （圣人，吾不得而见之矣。）— 7.25

He names his own faults as ordinary:

"Virtue not cultivated, learning not discussed, hearing what is right and being unable to shift to it, unable to change what is not good — these are my worries." （德之不修，学之不讲，闻义不能徙，不善不能改，是吾忧也。）— 7.3

And when a duke's minister asks a student what sort of man Confucius is, the student cannot answer. Confucius supplies the description himself:

"Why did you not say: he is the sort of man who, in his eagerness, forgets to eat; who in his gladness forgets his cares; who does not notice old age coming on." （女奚不曰：其为人也，发愤忘食，乐以忘忧，不知老之将至云尔。）— 7.18

Three participles. No achievement. Asked to say who he is, he gives a description with no completed accomplishment in it — only three things still going on.

What this run of evidence establishes, and what it does not

A record that costs the school something is harder to explain as veneration. A tradition constructing a sage does not have its founder decline the title, report himself unfinished, list his ordinary faults, and describe himself as still in the middle of trying. Every one of these gives away standing he could have kept.

What this raises is the likelihood that these are early or real. It does not settle who wrote them or when. We do not treat it as if it did, and where later Parts lean on this pattern, the same limit applies.

He also stops others from building him up

The removal is not only self-directed. When a student assembles a retinue of "retainers" during his illness — an honour due to a minister, which he was not — the reaction is severe:

"Long has You been carrying out this deception! To pretend I have retainers when I have none — whom would I be deceiving? Would I be deceiving Heaven?" （久矣哉，由之行诈也！无臣而为有臣，吾谁欺？欺天乎？）— 9.12

And earlier, when a high official praises him as nearly divine, he takes it apart on the spot:

"Does the Grand Steward know me? When I was young I was in low station, so I became capable in many menial things." （太宰知我乎？吾少也贱，故多能鄙事。）— 9.6

The claim was that his range of ability showed Heaven's endowment. His answer is that he was poor and had to learn things. The explanation offered is biographical and slightly deflating, and he supplies it himself.

2. He had no stock of answers

The method is stated once, plainly:

"Do I have knowledge? I do not. A common fellow asks me something, and my mind is empty — I knock at both ends of it and work it through to the finish." （吾有知乎哉？无知也。有鄙夫问于我，空空如也，我叩其两端而竭焉。）— 9.8

Three features, each doing work.

"My mind is empty." Nothing is retrieved. There is no prepared answer being matched to an incoming question — which is the difference between teaching and dispensing.

"I knock at both ends." The question is opened from its extremes, and worked through with the questioner. The questioner is present for the working, not handed the result.

"A common fellow." The method does not upgrade for better students. This matters more than it looks: a teacher who has a real method uses it on everyone; a teacher who has a doctrine dispenses simplified versions downward.

And the four negations

"Four things the Master had done away with: no conjecture, no insistence on certainty, no rigidity, no self-centring." （子绝四：毋意，毋必，毋固，毋我。）— 9.4

Four "do nots," and each rules out a way of foreclosing:
毋意no conjecturedo not fill the gap with a guess
毋必no insistence on certaintydo not treat the conclusion as settled
毋固no rigiditydo not hold it against revision
毋我no self-centringdo not make yourself the measure

These are negations, and the form is worth noting without over-reading it. A negation can mark a limit and leave the inside open. It can also be absolute prohibition. What makes these four read as the former is not their grammatical shape but what they rule out: each names a way of closing a question, and closing questions is the thing this reading finds him consistently declining to do.

(An earlier version of this work held that top-level principles must take negative form. That claim is withdrawn — see Volume Two, §7.)

3. The account from the other side

Book 9 contains the one sustained report from a student's position — Yan Hui describing what being taught by Confucius was like. It is set out in full in Volume One (§III), and the reading is not repeated here. What belongs in this Part is the pairing.

In the same book, two hands are visible side by side:

  • 9.11 — Yan Hui's account, which includes the admission that he could not keep up: "I have used myself up… though I would follow it, there is no way through."
  • 9.12 — Zilu's retinue, and the accusation of deception.

One student records the process honestly, including his own failure to complete it. Another dresses his teacher up and is called a fraud for it.

Set beside each other in one book, these are the two things that can be done with a teacher. And the Analects keeps both — including the one that makes a student look bad. That the compilers preserved 9.12 at all is part of what makes the early layer credible.

4. Book 10, and the limit of what a record holds

Book 10 is unlike anything else in the Analects. It records how he dressed, what he ate, how he sat, how he entered a gate, how he behaved in his village, at court, in a carriage. There is almost no teaching in it, and this book does not anchor on it.

But the reason matters, and it is not the reason usually given.

This is not the compilers failing. It is the limit of what the form can hold.

Every one of those acts carried a judgment — what does this situation, now, call for? — and that judgment moved with the person, the place, and the day. Written down, only the act survives. "He did not sit unless the mat was straight" preserves the act and loses the reading of the moment that produced it. The recorders did their work. This is as far as a conduct-log reaches.

And Book 10 makes no claim to be teaching. It does not present these as instructions. It records behaviour. There is nothing here to oppose — it simply cannot carry the reconstruction.

The one opening in the wall

Amid the catalogue, one line:

The stables burned. The Master, returning from court, said: "Was anyone hurt?" He did not ask about the horses. （厩焚。子退朝，曰：伤人乎？不问马。）— 10.17

In a period when horses were expensive and stablemen were not, the first question after a fire was about people.

Set against the rest of the book, this is the contrast that makes the point. The catalogue records what he always did. This records what he judged, once, in a situation nobody had a rule for. The first is what a text can hold. The second is what cultivation actually consists of — and it survives here only because someone happened to write down a question rather than a practice.

5. Withholding the word

Books 5 and 6 are largely assessments of people, and they run on a pattern that is easy to miss: he will not call anyone ren.

Asked whether one student is ren: "I do not know about his ren, but he could be put in charge of the levies." Another: "he could be made steward of a great house — I do not know about his ren." A third, the same (5.8). Of a man widely admired for loyalty, twice: "loyal, certainly. As for ren — I do not know" (5.19).

And of the student he rated highest, the most he will say is a duration:

"Hui — his heart does not depart from ren for three months at a time. The others reach it for a day, or a month, and that is all." （回也，其心三月不违仁，其余则日月至焉而已矣。）— 6.7

Even here it is a stretch of time, not a status.

This looks like severity. It is better read as a refusal to close. To certify someone as ren is to complete a judgment about them — to hand them a settled account of what they are. What he gives instead is specific and provisional: he can do this; about that I do not know.

And the same refusal covers himself: "Sage, and ren — how would I dare" (7.33). The word is withheld from everyone, including the speaker. A standard that no one is certified against is a standard nobody can be finished by.

6. What the seam yields

Six books, and three things come out of them that the later Parts rely on.

One: the method has no reserve. 9.8 states it, and the pattern of assessments confirms it — no stock of answers, no certifications, nothing held back for the advanced student. When he is accused of holding something back, he denies it flatly and stakes his character on it: "there is nothing I do that I do not share with you — that is who I am" (7.23).

Two: the standing is given away, repeatedly, from several directions. He declines the titles; he explains his abilities by his poor childhood; he stops a student from dressing him up; he tells students not to defer to him on what matters most. Each of these is a place where authority was available and not taken.

Three: the record has a floor, and Book 10 shows where it is. Conduct can be written; the judgment behind it cannot. This is not a complaint about the compilers — it is the reason a reconstruction like this one has to work from exchanges rather than from practices, and the reason so much of the later tradition, working from practices, arrived somewhere else.

Appendix · The chapters of Books 5–10

Every chapter, with its position and the reason for it.

Reading the entries. Anchor = this reading builds on it; (core) = load-bearing; (probable) / (weak) = admitted with lower confidence. Non-anchor is not a claim that a passage is wrong. Split judgment = units of one chapter judged separately. Set aside as contested = the sense cannot be settled.

Book 10 is registered by cluster rather than by chapter — see the note at its head.

Book 5 · 公冶长 Gongye Chang

A book of assessments — and the place where the method of assessing is on clearest display. He marries a daughter to an ex-convict, grades his own students to their faces, records a case where a student changed his mind for him, and reports that he is not better than the next man at anything except being willing to learn.

Two instruments run through it. Multi-axis assessment — a person is read on several scales at once and the scores are never combined. And withholding the word ren — asked six times whether various men have it, he declines six times, including about himself.

5.1

The Master said of Gongye Chang: "He can be given a wife. Though he was in fetters, it was not his crime." And he gave him his daughter. He said of Nan Rong: "When the state has the way, he will not be discarded; when it has not, he will escape punishment." And he gave him his elder brother's daughter.

子谓公冶长，「可妻也。虽在缧绁之中，非其罪也」。以其子妻之。子谓南容，「邦有道，不废；邦无道，免于刑戮」。以其兄之子妻之。

Anchor.

A judgment underwritten with an action: he gives his daughter to a man who has been in prison. It was not his crime — the reading went through the criminal record. He read the man, not the label.

The Nan Rong assessment is a prognosis — the method of 2.10 — and it reads in two directions at once: he will not be cast aside when things are well ordered, and he will not be destroyed when they are not. A capacity to survive both kinds of world. 11.6 supplies the file behind the prognosis (Nan Rong repeated the ode about the flawed white sceptre three times a day — that is, he watched his own speech).

A hard-to-fabricate feature: he assesses people and then backs the assessment with his own family.

5.2

The Master said of Zijian: "A noble person indeed, this man! But if Lu had no noble persons, where would he have got this from?"

子谓子贱，「君子哉若人！鲁无君子者，斯焉取斯」。

Anchor.

Two layers in one sentence. He praises Zijian — and praises more the environment that produced him. Virtue has a provenance; a noble person does not grow alone.

Structurally the same as 4.1 on the new reading: dwelling in ren is keeping close to those who treat people as ends, because that is how discernment is acquired.

(Zijian's later record in the histories is of governing Shanfu by sitting in his hall and playing the zither — the assessment was not idle praise.)

5.3

Zigong asked: "What about me?" The Master said: "You are a vessel." "What vessel?" "A sacrificial jade vessel."

子贡问曰：「赐也何如？」子曰：「女，器也。」曰：「何器也？」曰：「瑚琏也。」

Anchor (core).

Zigong asks for his own grade, and gets 2.12 delivered to his face: a noble person is not a vessel — you are a vessel.

Then a second dose. Asked which, he answers: the jade vessel used in the ancestral temple. The most precious vessel there is — and still a vessel.

Praise with a needle in it, and a needle wrapped in praise. Two calibrations in one exchange: you have not arrived at not-being-a-vessel, and you are not being written off.

And Zigong is exactly the man who most needs both halves — the school's ablest operator, most likely to stop at competence.

5.4

Someone said: "Yong has ren but is not eloquent." The Master said: "What use is eloquence? Those who meet others with a ready tongue are often disliked. I do not know about his ren — but what use is eloquence?"

或曰：「雍也仁而不佞。」子曰：「焉用佞？御人以口给，屡憎于人。不知其仁也，焉用佞？」

Anchor. Anonymous occasion.

What he objects to is not the praise but the "but." Someone has treated non-eloquence as a deficit. What use is eloquence? — a ready tongue is not a missing virtue; it is an instrument for handling people, and it earns dislike (with 1.3's clever words and 5.25's excessive deference: all three are managing people by manner, which is treating them as means).

"I do not know about his ren" is a concession, not a denial — he elsewhere says Yong could be given a ruler's seat (6.1). The point stands whether or not the ren question is settled.

5.5

The Master urged Qidiao Kai to take office. He replied: "I cannot yet be confident about this." The Master was pleased.

子使漆雕开仕。对曰：「吾斯之未能信。」子说。

Anchor. Fully occasioned.

A student declines a post offered by his teacher, on the ground that he does not yet judge himself ready — and the teacher is pleased.

What is he pleased at? That the student measured himself and came up short, and said so. The same value as you would ruin another man's son (11.24) and the smile at Zilu's boast (11.26): do not go out unripe.

A layer building a sage writes teachers whose students obey. It does not write the teacher recommending, the student refusing, and the teacher delighted.

5.6

The Master said: "The way does not prevail. I shall take a raft and float out to sea. The one who follows me — that will be You." Zilu heard it and was delighted. The Master said: "You is fonder of courage than I am. But there is nothing to make timber of."

子曰：「道不行，乘桴浮于海。从我者，其由与？」子路闻之喜。子曰：「由也好勇过我，无所取材。」

Anchor. Occasioned, two people.

"I shall take a raft and float out to sea" is self-mockery in a bad moment — the same register as the phoenix does not come (9.9) and the one who knows it cannot be done (14.38). "The one who follows me will be You" is half affection and half bait.

Zilu is delighted — he heard the naming and missed the irony. And the correction follows immediately.

The order matters: Zilu is delighted before his teacher names his fault. The praise came first and the needle after — which is the sequence used on someone who can take it.

5.7

Meng Wubo asked: "Is Zilu ren?" The Master said: "I do not know." Asked again, he said: "You could be put in charge of the levies of a state of a thousand chariots. Whether he is ren, I do not know." "What about Qiu?" "Qiu could be made steward of a town of a thousand households or of a great house. Whether he is ren, I do not know." "What about Chi?" "Chi, in his sash, could stand at court and converse with the guests. Whether he is ren, I do not know."

孟武伯问：「子路仁乎？」子曰：「不知也。」……「由也，千乘之国，可使治其赋也，不知其仁也。」……

Anchor (core). The methodological statement of the assessment books.

Three men, three answers, all built the same way: first a precise placement of capacity — Zilu on military levies, Ran Qiu on administration, Gongxi Chi on diplomatic reception, each given his exact range — and then, three times, whether he is ren, I do not know.

This is multi-axis assessment in pure form. The capability axis is filled in fully and accurately. The ren axis is suspended, every time.

Ren is not the premium grade of ability. It is a different dimension, and high ability does not convert into it.

Three identical closings, like a metronome at full volume.

5.8

"What a man Hui is! A single bamboo dish of rice, a single gourd of drink, living in a mean alley — others could not bear the misery of it, and Hui does not let his joy be altered. What a man Hui is!"

子曰：「贤哉回也！一箪食，一瓢饮，在陋巷，人不堪其忧，回也不改其乐。贤哉回也！」

Anchor (core). (Numbered 6.11 in some editions.)

The joy does not come from the circumstances, so the circumstances cannot take it away. With 7.16 (coarse food, water to drink, my bent arm for a pillow — joy lies therein) and 7.19.

The exclamation opens and closes the chapter — and exclamation is treated in this reading as a positive signal: the marker of feeling, not the flat assertion of a maxim.

5.9

The Master said to Zigong: "Which is better, you or Hui?" He replied: "How would I dare compare myself to Hui? Hui hears one thing and knows ten. I hear one and know two." The Master said: "You are not equal to him. I grant you — neither of us is equal to him."

子谓子贡曰：「女与回也孰愈？」对曰：「赐也何敢望回。回也闻一以知十，赐也闻一以知二。」子曰：「弗如也，吾与女弗如也。」

Anchor (core).

A person is measured by extension rate — one to ten, one to two. The same scale as tell you what has gone and you know what is coming (1.15) and keep the old warm and know the new (2.11).

The last clause has two readings. 与 as agree with: "You are not equal to him; I agree with you." Or 与 as and: "neither you nor I is equal to him."

On the second, the teacher places himself below a student — which a sanctifying layer could not have produced. On the first, he confirms a student's low self-rating to his face, without consolation — which is hardly easier to write.

And Zigong's own answer is honest without ornament. The record of the dose is as candid as the dose.

5.10

Zai Yu slept in the daytime. The Master said: "Rotten wood cannot be carved; a wall of dung cannot be plastered. What is the use of reproving Yu?" And: "At first, with people, I listened to their words and trusted their conduct. Now I listen to their words and watch their conduct. It was Yu who made me change."

宰予昼寝。子曰：「朽木不可雕也，粪土之墙不可杇也，于予与何诛？」子曰：「始吾于人也，听其言而信其行；今吾于人也，听其言而观其行。于予与改是。」

Anchor (core).

He records a revision of his own method, and credits it to a specific failure. From listen and trust to listen and watch — and he names the case that taught him: it was Yu who made me change.

A teacher reporting that his way of reading people used to be too credulous, and that a student cured him of it, is not easily explained by an intention to venerate.

And the anger is preserved — rotten wood, a wall of dung. A hagiographical portrait does not include the temper. This is the second honest embarrassment in this book (the first is Zilu at 5.6).

5.11

The Master said: "I have not seen a man who is truly firm." Someone replied: "Shen Cheng." The Master said: "Cheng has desires. How can he be firm?"

子曰：「吾未见刚者。」或对曰：「申枨。」子曰：「枨也欲，焉得刚？」

Anchor. I have not seen — calibrated — plus an anonymous exchange.

One word decides it: 欲, desire. Whoever wants something can be held by it, and so cannot be firm. Firmness is not a hard temperament; it is having nothing by which one can be gripped.

A quality is reclassified from disposition to position: what makes a man unbendable is that there is no purchase on him. Nothing he wants means nothing anyone can use.

5.12

Zigong said: "What I do not want others to do to me, I also wish not to do to others." The Master said: "Ci — that is not something you have reached."

子贡曰：「我不欲人之加诸我也，吾亦欲无加诸人。」子曰：「赐也，非尔所及也。」

Anchor (core), and one of the load-bearing chapters of this whole reading.

Zigong says something almost identical to the formula his teacher will later give him as a lifetime's word (15.24, what you do not want done to you, do not do to others). And he is told: that is not something you have reached.

Look at what actually differs. Zigong states a wish of his own — I also wish not to. The formula he is given later is a general rule — do not. A wish and a discipline are not the same distance from the ground.

And the chapter is a control case for the whole method. Here is a student producing a near-perfect saying, and the teacher declining it as premature. A tradition assembling doctrine keeps the good sentence and drops the deflation. This one kept both — including the part where the star student is told he is not there yet.

5.13

Zigong said: "Our Master's cultural teaching can be heard. Our Master's words on human nature and the way of Heaven cannot be heard."

子贡曰：「夫子之文章，可得而闻也；夫子之言性与天道，不可得而闻也。」

Anchor (core). A student's testimony to his teacher's silence.

This is not Zigong complaining that he missed the lectures. It is a report that there were none: there was no systematic course on human nature and the way of Heaven.

This book uses the chapter as a gate. Any passage attributed to Confucius that lays out a system of nature and Heaven has to get past this testimony first. (It is the instrument used on 17.2.)

With 7.21 — he did not speak of prodigies, feats of strength, disorder, or spirits — and 9.1 — he seldom spoke of profit, of fate, or of ren.

5.14

When Zilu had heard something and had not yet been able to put it into practice, his only fear was that he would hear something else.

子路有闻，未之能行，唯恐有闻。

Anchor. A high-quality observation, and hard to fabricate.

He has heard a thing and not yet done it, and what he dreads is learning another. Knowing has been pushed so far behind doing that a surplus of knowing over doing frightens him.

This is the positive face of Zilu's courage — bold to act, therefore ashamed of idle knowledge — the same root as his fault (fonder of courage than I am). The recorder caught an inward movement, and caught it accurately.

5.15

Zigong asked: "Why was Kong Wenzi given the title 'Cultured'?" The Master said: "He was quick and fond of learning, and not ashamed to ask those below him. That is why he was called 'Cultured'."

子贡问曰：「孔文子何以谓之『文』也？」子曰：「敏而好学，不耻下问，是以谓之『文』也。」

Anchor. Occasioned by Zigong's question — and the question has an edge, since Kong Wenzi's private conduct was disreputable.

The answer does not defend the man's character and does not withdraw the title. It settles what "Cultured" means in this case: quick, fond of learning, and not ashamed to ask those beneath him.

Multi-axis again: private conduct on one axis, love of learning on another, and they do not cancel.

And "not ashamed to ask those below" is another "that is what it means" redefinition: the honorific is earned by asking, not by literary polish.

5.16

The Master said of Zichan: "He had four of the ways of a noble person: in his own conduct, respectful; in serving his superior, reverent; in caring for the people, generous; in employing the people, just."

子谓子产，「有君子之道四焉：其行己也恭，其事上也敬，其养民也惠，其使民也义」。

Anchor.

Four items, and normally a list of four would count against a passage here. What removes the objection is that there is a named man and a documented record of governing (Zichan kept Zheng intact between Jin and Chu for a generation). This is not a list of virtues in the abstract; it is a reading of one administrator's judgment on four faces.

And the last two turn outward to the governed: generous in caring for the people, just in employing them. The endpoint is what the people receive, not how they are managed.

5.17

The Master said: "Yan Pingzhong was good at keeping company with others. Long acquainted, he still showed them respect."

子曰：「晏平仲善与人交，久而敬之。」

Anchor.

The test of a friendship is time — respect that does not decay with familiarity.

And this is the time dimension of 2.10 again: against concealment and performance, time is the solvent. Anyone can manage a while; few can keep it up. With 5.10 (watch the conduct) and 5.21 (the foolishness that could not be kept up), all three are the same remedy: let it run.

5.18

The Master said: "Zang Wenzhong housed a great tortoise, with hill-shaped capitals and duckweed-patterned beams. How can he be counted wise?"

子曰：「臧文仲居蔡，山节藻棁，何如其知也？」

Anchor. A closing question — a positive signal, leaving the verdict to the hearer.

Zang Wenzhong kept a divination tortoise and decorated its housing with ornament proper to the Son of Heaven: usurpation. And one question strips him of his reputation for wisdom: how is that wise?

知 is thereby reclassified — from cleverness to the sense not to overstep (with 2.17's that is knowing and 8.9's boundary on knowing).

An assessment of a man that is really a rectification of a name — a page of the usurpation dossier, filed in this book. And a question rather than a verdict: whether he counts as wise is left with the reader.

5.19

Zizhang asked: "The chief minister Ziwen was three times made chief minister and showed no delight; three times dismissed, and showed no resentment. He always informed the incoming minister of the outgoing administration. What of him?" The Master said: "He was loyal." "Was he ren?" "I do not know — how should that amount to ren?" … "Was he ren?" "I do not know — how should that amount to ren?"

子张问曰：「令尹子文……何如？」子曰：「忠矣。」曰：「仁矣乎？」曰：「未知，焉得仁？」……

Anchor (core). The textbook chapter for multi-axis assessment.

Zizhang brings two cases — one man faithful in office, one man clean in his person — and twice asks the same follow-up: but was he ren?

Twice the same shape of answer: a precise virtue is granted — loyal, clean — and the ren axis is suspended.

Loyalty is not ren. Purity is not ren. The virtues are separate coordinates: they do not sum into ren, and they do not convert into each other.

With 5.7 this makes a pair: there, capability filled in and ren suspended; here, virtue filled in and ren suspended. Read together, they show that the suspension is systematic and not evasive.

5.20

Ji Wenzi thought three times before acting. Hearing of it, the Master said: "Twice will do."

季文子三思而后行。子闻之，曰：「再，斯可矣。」

Anchor.

Counter-intuitive, and it clears the bar on exactly that ground. Deliberation is a safe virtue to praise. He puts a ceiling on it: three times over is not caution but the breeding-ground of calculation — turning a thing over and over is how one finds the way to avoid it.

A layer building a sage pushes prudence upward without limit. It does not write "twice will do."

Same scale as 2.15 (learning and thinking must balance) and 11.16 (going too far is as bad as falling short): a virtue has a measure, and past it, it reverses.

5.21

The Master said: "Ning Wuzi, when the state had the way, was wise; when the state had not the way, he was a fool. His wisdom can be matched. His foolishness cannot."

子曰：「宁武子，邦有道则知，邦无道则愚。其知可及也，其愚不可及也。」

Anchor. A two-state assessment — the prognostic method of 2.10.

And the blade is the second half: the capacity to play the fool in a bad age is harder to acquire than the capacity to shine in a good one.

Note what kind of foolishness this is. Not the foolishness of a man who cannot see — the foolishness of a man who has recognized that the state has lost the way, and dims himself deliberately. Recognition comes first; concealment is chosen. So it is high-order knowing. (The same character, two entirely different valences, decided by which direction it runs — as with shame at 2.3.)

5.22

The Master, in Chen, said: "Let us go home! Let us go home! The young men of my district are wild and hasty, accomplished in their patterning, but they do not know how to trim themselves."

子在陈，曰：「归与！归与！吾党之小子狂简，斐然成章，不知所以裁之。」

Anchor. Self-report, in his own voice.

In Chen — in difficulty — and what he thinks of is home, and specifically the young men there: brilliant, already producing something with a shape to it, and not knowing how to cut themselves back.

"Let us go home" is a teacher's pull toward material that can still be worked. Talent needs trimming, and trimming people is his business.

The same character 裁 as in 5.6 ("nothing to make timber of") and the same concern as 11.22 (pushing one forward, holding one back): talent unaccompanied by trimming is only wildness.

5.23

The Master said: "Bo Yi and Shu Qi did not keep old wrongs in mind, and so resentment was rare in them."

子曰：「伯夷、叔齐不念旧恶，怨是用希。」

Anchor. Rare — the calibrating word.

Read against 4.12 (act with an eye to profit and you meet much resentment): the small man is sticky — a loss is an entry in a ledger awaiting settlement. Not keeping old wrongs in mind is not opening the account at all.

So this is not one virtue among others. It is what the difference between the two positions looks like on the resentment axis. The causal chain is short and exact: less resentment follows from not holding on.

5.24

The Master said: "Who says Weisheng Gao is straight? Someone begged vinegar of him; he begged it of a neighbour and gave it to the man."

子曰：「孰谓微生高直？或乞醯焉，乞诸其邻而与之。」

Anchor (core). The microscope of assessment.

A trivial incident. A man is asked for vinegar, has none, quietly gets some from next door, and hands it over. The world calls that obliging.

He reads it as not straight. The man had none, did not say he had none, and went round the back to acquire the credit for generosity.

Straightness is what is, is; what is not, is not — the behavioural form of 2.17's when you know, hold that you know; when you do not, allow that you do not. One turn of the hand, and it was already lost.

Straightness is thereby reclassified: from generosity in conduct to non-embellishment of the facts.

The hard-to-fabricate quality is high — reading a whole man off a cup of vinegar is not what a compiler of maxims produces.

5.25

The Master said: "Clever words, an ingratiating face, excessive deference — Zuoqiu Ming was ashamed of these, and I too am ashamed of them. To conceal resentment and befriend the man — Zuoqiu Ming was ashamed of it, and I too am ashamed of it."

子曰：「巧言、令色、足恭，左丘明耻之，丘亦耻之。匿怨而友其人，左丘明耻之，丘亦耻之。」

Anchor.

Clever words and an ingratiating face return from 1.3, joined by excessive deference and by concealed resentment behind a friendly face — all four are the management of others by manner, which is to treat them as means.

But note the form of the judgment. He does not issue a general prohibition. He says: I too am ashamed — placing himself beside another man rather than above the reader.

A shared shame is offered, not a rule imposed. I am ashamed of it — and you? The judgment is left with the hearer.

5.26

Yan Yuan and Jilu were attending him. The Master said: "Why not each of you tell me what you are set on?" Zilu said: "I should like carriages and horses and light furs, to share with my friends, and not to mind their wearing them out." Yan Yuan said: "I should like never to boast of what is good in me, never to make a show of my labours." Zilu said: "We should like to hear what you are set on." The Master said: "That the old should be at ease, that friends should trust me, that the young should be cherished."

颜渊、季路侍。子曰：「盍各言尔志？」……子曰：「老者安之，朋友信之，少者怀之。」

Anchor (core). Three aspirations side by side — and the grammar of each is the reading.

One question. Three positions, three grammars.

Zilu — positive and concrete: carriages, horses, furs, shared with friends. Generosity in things.

Yan Hui — double negation: never boast, never make a show. Not even the good and the labour are claimed. Inward, and in negative form.

Confucius — pure description of an effect: the old at ease, friends trusting, the young cherished. Not "I would do X." A state of affairs that a certain position produces — the same shape as the empire turns to ren and keeps its place and the stars turn about it. The subject has almost disappeared from the sentence.

Three men, and the form of each answer places him.

5.27

The Master said: "It is hopeless! I have not yet seen anyone who could see his own faults and bring a case against himself within."

子曰：「已矣乎！吾未见能见其过而内自讼者也。」

Anchor. An exclamation, a calibrated I have not seen, and the first person.

内自讼 — to prosecute oneself inwardly. Not to notice a fault and dress it, but to bring the charge.

This pushes the threshold of correction one level below 4.17's examine yourself within: examining is noticing; prosecuting is indicting.

And "I have not seen" is an empiricist's lament — this step is very hard and he has not met anyone who takes it. 7.31 is its positive face: I am fortunate — when I err, someone is sure to know it.

5.28

The Master said: "In a hamlet of ten households there will certainly be someone as loyal and trustworthy as I am. There will not be someone as fond of learning."

子曰：「十室之邑，必有忠信如丘者焉，不如丘之好学也。」

Anchor (core). A precise piece of self-calibration.

Loyalty and good faith: a hamlet of ten households will produce someone my equal. He does not claim his own virtues as distinguishing.

Fond of learning: only here is there a difference.

He measures himself inside ordinary human nature. The virtues are common stock; the willingness to keep learning is the only variable he claims.

A sanctifying layer writes Heaven has endowed him as a sage — which is what Zigong said at 9.6, and was taken apart on the spot. It does not write "I am no more loyal than anyone else; I am only fonder of learning."

Book 5 in sum. Ten core anchors — the highest concentration so far — because this is where assessment is done in the open and the method is visible in the doing.

Two findings carry forward. Ren is suspended systematically (5.7, 5.19), and it is suspended for himself as well — a standard nobody is certified against is a standard nobody can be finished by. And the record keeps what costs the school something: the temper at Zai Yu, the delight of Zilu that missed the joke, the star student told he is not there yet, the teacher revising his own method and naming the student who forced it.

Book 6 · 雍也 Yong Ye

Assessment continues, and then the book turns. The first half grades men — Zhonggong, Yan Hui, Ran Qiu, Min Ziqian. The second half contains the positive definition of ren (6.30), the redefinition of learning (6.3), and the dismantling of the commonest excuse there is (6.12).

Two chapters here are among the most load-bearing in the whole work: 6.30, where ren is finally defined rather than withheld, and 6.12, where "I haven't the strength" is taken apart.

6.1

The Master said: "Yong could be given a seat facing south."

子曰：「雍也可使南面。」

Anchor.

Facing south is the ruler's position. To say of a student that he could hold it is the highest reading on the capability scale.

And it corroborates 12.2, where Zhonggong asks about ren and receives an operable scaffolding — out of doors, behave as though receiving an important guest. A man who could face south is given a form of ren that can actually be run.

6.2

Zhonggong asked about Zisang Bozi. The Master said: "He will do — he is simple." Zhonggong said: "To dwell in reverence and act with simplicity in overseeing the people — would that not do? But to dwell in simplicity and act with simplicity — is that not too simple?" The Master said: "Yong's words are right."

仲弓问子桑伯子。子曰：「可也，简。」仲弓曰：「居敬而行简……不亦可乎？居简而行简，无乃大简乎？」子曰：「雍之言然。」

Anchor (core). A two-layer chapter — dialogue, then the teacher's concession.

Zhonggong distinguishes two grades of "simple." Reverent within and simple in action — simplicity as not troubling the people with unnecessary business. Simple within and simple in action — simplicity as laziness.

And the teacher says: Yong is right. He is corrected by a student's distinction and accepts it on the spot.

With "it is Shang who draws me out" (3.8) and "neither of us is equal to him" (5.9): three occasions on which a student lifted the teacher, and the teacher said so.

6.3

Duke Ai asked: "Which of your students loves learning?" Confucius replied: "There was Yan Hui, who loved learning. He did not transfer his anger; he did not repeat an error. Unfortunately his life was short and he died. Now there is no one — I have not heard of anyone who loves learning."

哀公问：「弟子孰为好学？」子曰：「有颜回者好学，不迁怒，不贰过。不幸短命死矣。今也则亡。」

Anchor (core). The anchor for the redefinition of learning.

A duke asks who loves learning. The answer contains no books. It contains two negations: he did not transfer anger — his anger did not move onto other people or other things; he did not repeat an error — the same fault did not happen twice.

Learning is thereby redefined as self-transformation at the level of emotion and error, with no reference to texts whatsoever.

This is the definitional anchor for 学 in the whole book — the general form is at 1.14, and the ordering is at 1.6 (if energy remains, then study the texts).

What the later narrowing did to this word can be measured against this chapter.

6.4

Zihua was sent on a mission to Qi, and Ran Qiu asked for grain for his mother. The Master said: "Give her a fu." He asked for more. "Give her a yu." Ran Qiu gave her five bing. The Master said: "When Chi went to Qi, he had well-fed horses and light furs. I have heard that the noble person helps out the needy; he does not add to the wealth of the rich."

子华使于齐，冉子为其母请粟……子曰：「赤之适齐也，乘肥马，衣轻裘。吾闻之也：君子周急不继富。」

Anchor. Fully occasioned — and a principle demonstrated in the act.

Ran Qiu asks for grain for the mother of a student on a mission; the amount granted is small; he gives ten times as much anyway.

The teacher does not rebuke the disobedience. He states the principle: Chi went to Qi with fat horses and light furs — the man is not short of anything.

周急不继富 — help the urgent, do not top up the rich — six characters that draw the boundary of relief. (It is the other face of 周 in 2.14: reaching everywhere that is genuinely in need, and not spilling over where there is none.)

6.5

When Yuan Si was made steward, he was given nine hundred measures of grain, and declined. The Master said: "Do not! Could you not give it to the people of your neighbourhood?"

原思为之宰，与之粟九百，辞。子曰：「毋！以与尔邻里乡党乎！」

Anchor. Occasioned.

Yuan Si refuses his salary, and is told not to.

What the chapter shows is that an action has more than one direction. Refusing the stipend is a kind of inward keeping-clean — declining in order to preserve oneself. The teacher supplies the other direction: give it to your neighbourhood — outward distribution.

The principle (do not be greedy for salary) holds either way. What is being corrected is the assumption that living by it can only mean refusing. Take what is due, and pass on what exceeds your need.

6.6

The Master said of Zhonggong: "The calf of a plough-ox, but red and with straight horns — though men might not wish to use it, would the spirits of the hills and streams reject it?"

子谓仲弓曰：「犁牛之子骍且角，虽欲勿用，山川其舍诸？」

Anchor (core). An image, left unexplained.

A plough-ox is a working animal, of no distinction. Its calf, if red-coated and straight-horned, meets the standard for sacrificial use. Zhonggong's father was of low station.

The claim is that quality attests itself and does not run through birth. Men might decline to use the animal on account of its parentage; the spirits would not.

This is the case-by-case form of in teaching, no categories — and the direct counter-instance to any reading of 17.3 that fixes people by birth. A man is released from the label of his origin, and read on what he is.

6.7

The Master said: "Hui — his heart does not depart from ren for three months at a time. The others reach it for a day or a month, and that is all."

子曰：「回也，其心三月不违仁，其余则日月至焉而已矣。」

Anchor (core). Ren measured as duration.

Three months of not departing against occasional arrival. What is measured is how long the structure is held — the same scale as not for the space of a single meal (4.5).

Note that 而已矣 here is not a seal. It does not close the question, as it does at 4.15. It calibrates: the others manage it now and then, and that is the honest state of things.

6.8

Ji Kangzi asked: "Is Zhongyou fit to be given office?" The Master said: "You is decisive. What difficulty would he have in office?" "Is Ci fit?" "Ci is perceptive. What difficulty would he have?" "Is Qiu fit?" "Qiu is accomplished. What difficulty would he have?"

季康子问：「仲由可使从政也与？」曰：「由也果，于从政乎何有？」……

Anchor. Three questions, three answers — the cross-section form of 5.7.

One character apiece: decisive, perceptive, accomplished. Each man placed by his own quality, and each declared fit.

Which is the finding worth drawing out: fitness for office is not one type. Three different men, three different strengths, all three suitable. His understanding of talent is plural.

And the closing question — what difficulty would he have? — leaves Ji Kangzi to think it through himself.

6.9

The Ji family sent to make Min Ziqian steward of Bi. Min Ziqian said: "Decline it for me politely. If anyone comes for me again, I shall certainly be on the banks of the Wen."

季氏使闵子骞为费宰。闵子骞曰：「善为我辞焉！如有复我者，则吾必在汶上矣。」

Anchor. Occasioned.

The whole judgment rests on who the Ji family are. They are the first of the three houses that had usurped the authority of Lu — the eight rows of dancers, the sacrifice at Mount Tai, the expulsion of the duke. Without that, the chapter is only "Min Ziqian did not want a job," and there is no discernment in it at all.

Bi was the Ji family's private domain, and the refusal is absolute: decline it properly for me — and if they come again, I shall have left the state.

6.10

Bo Niu was ill. The Master went to ask after him, took his hand through the window, and said: "We are losing him — it is fate. That such a man should have such an illness! That such a man should have such an illness!"

伯牛有疾，子问之，自牖执其手，曰：「亡之，命矣夫！斯人也而有斯疾也！斯人也而有斯疾也！」

Anchor (core). Grief, unsmoothed.

No instruction. No consolation. No theodicy. Only the pain and the incomprehension, and the sentence said twice.

It is fate is not an explanation — it is where explanation stops (with not yet knowing life, how should one know death, 11.12; and he seldom spoke of fate, 9.1). Fate here is the place one halts, not the answer one gives.

A sanctified portrait has no room for this. It is here.

6.11

The Master said: "What a man Hui is! A single bamboo dish of rice, a single gourd of drink, living in a mean alley — others could not bear the misery of it, and Hui does not let his joy be altered. What a man Hui is!"

子曰：「贤哉回也！一箪食，一瓢饮，在陋巷，人不堪其忧，回也不改其乐。贤哉回也！」

Anchor (core). (See 5.8, where the same chapter appears in some editions.)

The joy has its source inside, so the circumstances cannot reach it. With 7.16 (coarse rice, water to drink, my bent arm for a pillow — joy lies therein).

With 6.3 and 6.7, this makes the portrait of Yan Hui: he did not transfer anger or repeat an error; he held to ren for three months at a stretch; and his joy did not depend on his conditions.

6.12

Ran Qiu said: "It is not that I do not delight in your way — my strength is insufficient." The Master said: "One whose strength is insufficient gives out along the road. You have drawn a line."

冉求曰：「非不说子之道，力不足也。」子曰：「力不足者，中道而废。今女画。」

Anchor (core). The anchor for removing thresholds.

The excuse is dissected in one sentence. Genuine insufficiency shows up as collapse partway along. What Ran Qiu has done is draw a line before setting out.

Not: your ability falls short. But: you have set the boundary yourself.

With I want ren, and ren is here (7.30) and ren proceeds from oneself (12.1): all three remove a threshold that was self-installed.

And 4.6 corroborates it from the other side — I have not seen one whose strength was insufficient. Here the excuse is taken apart structurally; there, by observation.

6.13

The Master said to Zixia: "Be a ru of the noble kind, not a ru of the small kind."

子谓子夏曰：「女为君子儒，无为小人儒。」

Anchor (core). Named dosage — and the school's self-criticism.

Ru is what the school itself was called. He divides it in two: the noble kind — learning as becoming, growing oneself — and the small kind — learning as an instrument for reputation and salary, which is to be colonized by external estimation.

He is not attacking rival schools here. He is telling one of his own best students that his own trade can be practised in a way that hollows it out.

6.14

Ziyou was steward of Wucheng. The Master said: "Have you got hold of a man there?" He said: "There is Tantai Mieming, who does not take short cuts, and who has never come to my quarters except on official business."

子游为武城宰。子曰：「女得人焉尔乎？」曰：「有澹台灭明者，行不由径，非公事，未尝至于偃之室也。」

Anchor. A two-layer chapter.

The first question about governing is whether you have found the right people (with 2.19, 2.20).

And the man is identified by two small things. He does not take short cuts — literally, he does not cut across; he goes the proper way even walking. He has never been to my quarters except on business — he does not use the back door.

Character read off two trivia. The microscope again (compare the vinegar at 5.24).

6.15

The Master said: "Meng Zhifan does not boast. He was covering the retreat, and as he was about to enter the gate he whipped his horse and said: 'It was not that I dared to be last — the horse would not go forward.'"

子曰：「孟之反不伐。奔而殿，将入门，策其马，曰：『非敢后也，马不进也。』」

Anchor.

He held the rearguard — the most dangerous position in a rout — and at the gate he attributed it to a slow horse.

Credit that was his, assigned to something outside himself. This is the live case of never boast of what is good in me (5.26, Yan Hui's aspiration): not claiming the merit is not letting the merit become an asset one holds.

6.16

The Master said: "Without the eloquence of Zhu Tuo, and with the looks of Song Zhao — it is hard to escape in the present age."

子曰：「不有祝鮀之佞，而有宋朝之美，难乎免于今之世矣！」

Non-anchor.

No subject of comment, no background — and the register does not fit. Hard to escape in the present age is a general lament about the times.

And there is a counter-instance. Cut off between Chen and Cai, provisions gone and his followers ill, he sang to the lute and told Zilu that the noble person may certainly find himself in want (15.1). A man who took that in that circumstance is unlikely to have produced this kind of generalized indignation.

(This chapter was formerly opposed in this work, on the ground of the register. That was withdrawn: it advances no structure contrary to what this reading finds, and being uncharacteristic is not a reason to oppose. It simply cannot anchor.)

6.17

The Master said: "Who can go out except by the door? Why does no one go by this way?"

子曰：「谁能出不由户？何莫由斯道也？」

Anchor. Two questions — positive signals — and a structural definition of the way.

The door is the image and the way is the point: as a door is what one must pass through to go out, the way is what conduct must pass through.

And nothing is said about what the way contains. A single household image establishes that it cannot be gone around — a structural claim about the way as the manner of operating that must be passed through, not a body of content.

6.18

The Master said: "When substance overcomes refinement, one is rustic. When refinement overcomes substance, one is a clerk. Refinement and substance in balance — then a noble person."

子曰：「质胜文则野，文胜质则史。文质彬彬，然后君子。」

Anchor. The two-way axis again — and this time both errors are named.

Too much substance: rough. Too much polish: a functionary going through forms. Neither side is safe, and the calibration corrects in both directions (with losing the person / losing the words, 15.8, and labour lost / perilous, 2.15).

Structurally: 文 is outward operation, close to ritual; 质 is what is inwardly there, the plain ground of 3.8. Neither alone is the thing.

6.19

The Master said: "A person lives by being straight. If the crooked lives, it is by luck that he escapes."

子曰：「人之生也直，罔之生也幸而免。」

Anchor. The structural definition of straightness.

To live as a human being is to be straight — which, per 5.24, means not embellishing what is the case. Crookedness is a deviation from that, and its survival is fortune, not soundness.

And note the calibration: 幸 — by luck. The claim is not that the crooked must perish. It is that when they do not, luck is what did it. (Same word as I am fortunate, 7.31.)

6.20

The Master said: "Those who know it are not equal to those who love it. Those who love it are not equal to those who take joy in it."

子曰：「知之者不如好之者，好之者不如乐之者。」

Anchor (core). Three grades, and a ladder of internalization.

To know it: the thing is outside you — you have information about an object. To take joy in it: it has become your own joy — you and it are no longer separate.

Which is precisely the movement from means to end. To take joy in something is to hold it for its own sake — the object is no longer instrumental to anything.

The ladder is the internal form of what the rest of this book describes structurally.

6.21

The Master said: "To those above the middling sort, one may speak of the higher things. To those below the middling sort, one may not."

子曰：「中人以上，可以语上也；中人以下，不可以语上也。」

Anchor. A second-order statement of method — the absence of an occasion is not a negative signal here.

This is not "do not teach them." It is "do not teach out of order" (with I do not open the way for those not struggling, 7.8, and the opposite answers at 11.22).

And on this reading the second clause is a restraint on the teacher, not a limit on the student: pouring a higher-order account into someone who has not got there is colonization — they receive a formula they cannot yet own.

6.22

Fan Chi asked about knowing. The Master said: "Attend to what is right for the people; respect the spirits and keep them at a distance — that may be called knowing." He asked about ren. "The one with ren puts the difficulty first and the gain after — that may be called ren."

樊迟问知。子曰：「务民之义，敬鬼神而远之，可谓知也。」问仁。曰：「仁者先难而后获，可谓仁也。」

Anchor (core). Two questions, two fitted answers — and Fan Chi, who is placed lower, gets the most substantial, most operable versions.

Knowing: put your effort into what is fitting for people, and hold the spirits in respect at a distance — which is to keep the boundary between the knowable and the not (with 11.12; 7.21). Knowing is keeping the boundary, not crossing into what cannot be known.

And ren: difficulty first, gain after. The order is the whole content — whoever takes the gain first has already made the other party a means.

6.23

The Master said: "The knowing delight in water; those with ren delight in mountains. The knowing are active; those with ren are still. The knowing find joy; those with ren endure."

子曰：「知者乐水，仁者乐山；知者动，仁者静；知者乐，仁者寿。」

Anchor.

Not a list of attributes but two portraits in natural images — water for the mobile and adaptive, mountain for the settled and weighty. Left unexplained; the images do their own work.

Read with 6.22, this is the summary of the two paths that Fan Chi asked about separately.

6.24

The Master said: "Qi, with one change, would come to Lu. Lu, with one change, would come to the way."

子曰：「齐一变，至于鲁；鲁一变，至于道。」

Anchor.

A judgment of distance and direction, not a ranking of the two states. Qi — which after Guan Zhong ran on hegemony and results — is two changes from the way. Lu, which preserved more of the Zhou forms, is one.

With 2.23 (what is dropped and added) and 3.14 (following Zhou because it looked back on two dynasties): the interest is always in the direction of change, never in the prestige of a state.

6.25

The Master said: "A gu that is not a gu — is it a gu? Is it a gu?"

子曰：「觚不觚，觚哉！觚哉！」

Non-anchor.

A ritual vessel that no longer has the shape of one, and the name repeated twice. The lament about name and reality parting could be read as an image for rectification — but there is no subject of comment and nothing it is said about. Stripped of its occasion, what remains is a general complaint that things are not what they were.

(This chapter was formerly opposed here on the ground that it could easily be filled with reactionary content once emptied of reference. That was withdrawn: the possibility of misuse is not evidence, and in fact no such use was ever made of it — the line is too empty to fill. See Volume Two, §7, discipline 3.)

6.26

Zai Wo asked: "If someone with ren were told that a man had fallen into a well, would he go down after him?" The Master said: "Why would he do that? A noble person can be made to go, but not to be trapped; he can be deceived, but not fooled."

宰我问曰：「仁者，虽告之曰：『井有仁焉。』其从之也？」子曰：「何为其然也？君子可逝也，不可陷也；可欺也，不可罔也。」

Anchor (core).

Zai Wo sets a trap — as he does at 3.21 and 17.21; he specializes in awkward questions, and the school kept them all. The trap: is the person of ren someone who can be lured to his death by an appeal to ren?

The answer separates two pairs. Can be made to go, cannot be trapped: he will act to save someone, and he will not walk into a pit. Can be deceived, cannot be fooled: he can be misled about a fact; he cannot be talked out of his judgment.

Which settles something important: ren is not gullibility, and it does not suspend the faculty that reads situations.

6.27

The Master said: "A noble person, broadly learned in culture and bound in by ritual, may thereby not go astray."

子曰：「君子博学于文，约之以礼，亦可以弗畔矣夫。」

Anchor. The two-way axis in another form: broad outward, bound inward.

Taking widely from outside, and gathering it in — breadth that does not disperse (compare learning without thinking is labour lost, 2.15).

And the closing is calibrated: may thereby not go astray — not "will reach the way." A negative outcome, modestly stated, with the particle 矣夫 carrying the sigh.

This is the phrase Yan Hui uses at 9.11 to describe what was done to him: broadened me with culture, bound me in with ritual. The method described here is the method he reports receiving.

6.28

The Master saw Nanzi, and Zilu was displeased. The Master swore: "If I have done wrong, may Heaven abandon me! May Heaven abandon me!"

子见南子，子路不说。夫子矢之曰：「予所否者，天厌之！天厌之！」

Anchor (core). The teacher's discomfort, on the record.

Nanzi was the consort of Duke Ling of Wei — politically manipulative, and of notorious private reputation. Zilu's displeasure is entirely understandable. And his teacher swears an oath to him, twice.

On the reading taken here, the visit follows from the position: anyone may prove to be cultivable, and one does not find out without going. (With 7.29, the boy from Hu village: I approve his coming forward; I do not vouch for what he does afterwards — people are not refused in advance.)

And what makes the chapter valuable is not the reasoning but the preservation. A student sulking at his teacher, and the teacher reduced to swearing — twice — is not what a school with an image to protect writes down.

6.29

The Master said: "The virtue that is the mean in the ordinary — how perfect it is! And it has been rare among people for a long time."

子曰：「中庸之为德也，其至矣乎！民鲜久矣。」

Split judgment.

The first half anchors. 庸 is the ordinary, the everyday; 中 is not leaning. The mean in the ordinary is: not falling to either side, and doing so as daily practice — not as a rare feat.

It is not compromise. Not the arithmetic middle of two contents, but the refusal to be captured by either end — the same shape as no predisposition for and none against (4.10) and refinement and substance in balance (6.18).

The second half — rare among people for a long time — is suspect as a later addition: a general complaint about the age, in the register that this reading treats as the students' rather than the teacher's.

6.30

Zigong said: "Suppose someone conferred benefits widely on the people and could bring help to the multitude — what of that? Could it be called ren?" The Master said: "What has that to do with ren? It would surely be sagehood — even Yao and Shun fell short of it. As for ren: wishing to stand, one helps others to stand; wishing to arrive, one helps others to arrive. To be able to take what is near at hand as the analogy — that may be called the method of ren."

子贡曰：「如有博施于民而能济众，何如？可谓仁乎？」子曰：「何事于仁，必也圣乎！尧舜其犹病诸……夫仁者，己欲立而立人，己欲达而达人。能近取譬，可谓仁之方也已。」

Anchor (core). The positive definition of ren, and the hinge of this whole reading.

Zigong proposes greatness: universal benefit, help to the multitude. Is that ren?

The first move deflates it. What has that to do with ren? That would be sagehood — and even Yao and Shun fell short. Scale is removed from the definition. How many people you reached is not the measure — and no one has ever completed the large version anyway.

The second move defines it, and the definition is small and exact:

Wishing to stand, help others to stand. Wishing to arrive, help others to arrive.

Not: give them what you have. But: what you want for yourself — the standing, the arriving — want that for them, and act so that they get there. The other person's standing is the end, and it is their standing, not yours extended.

And the closing calibration is exact: to take what is near at hand as the analogy — that may be called the method of ren. Not "that is ren." The method. Even here, where he finally answers, he issues a qualified certificate.

Book 6 in sum. Thirteen core anchors — the densest book so far.

And three of them carry the rest of the work. 6.3 redefines learning as self-transformation, with no books in it. 6.12 takes apart the excuse of insufficient strength and finds a self-drawn line under it. And 6.30 finally states what ren is — after twenty-odd chapters of declining to say who has it — and states it as a movement toward another person's standing, with the scale deliberately removed.

Book 7 · 述而 Shu Er

The densest book in the Analects, and the centre of this reading. Twenty-seven of its thirty-seven chapters anchor, and not one of them is a school clause or a student's summary. It is almost entirely first-person: what he did, what he refused, what he had not managed, what he was afraid of.

And it is where the case against his own sanctification is concentrated. He declines innate knowledge (7.19), declines sagehood (7.33), reports himself unaccomplished (7.32), states that he has no secret teaching (7.23), and describes himself with three present participles and no achievements (7.18).

7.1

"Transmitting and not composing; trusting in and loving the ancients — in this I venture to compare myself to our old Peng."

子曰：「述而不作，信而好古，窃比于我老彭。」

Anchor (core). The methodological head of the book.

Not modesty about originality. A methodological consistency.

Writing cannot fit itself to the reader in real time. A fixed text says the same sentence to everyone; it cannot test whether this reader has reached the point of struggling, and it cannot stop where stopping is called for.

That is a structural limitation of writing relative to being in the room — not a verdict that writing is itself colonization. A book can state its conditions of application, keep branches open, show counter-cases, say plainly that it is revisable, and decline to require agreement. This book is attempting that. So we do not conclude that a cultivator must not write, and we do not claim this is the only historical sense of the line.

The misreading we oppose: this has been taken as a conservative charter — transmit only, invent nothing; whatever is ancient is to be followed. His own words are against it: he names rigidity as a fault to be cured (14.32), lists it among the four things done away with (9.4), holds the young in awe (9.23), describes what each age dropped and added (2.23), and says he is neither committed to a course nor against one (18.8).

"Transmitting" is keeping the dispensary and declining to pre-mix one prescription for everyone.

7.2

"To hold things silently in mind; to learn without tiring; to teach others without weariness — what of these is in me?"

子曰：「默而识之，学而不厌，诲人不倦，何有于我哉？」

Anchor (core). Three self-reports, closing on a question.

All three are processes and none is an accomplishment. Holding silently — taking in without having to put it out (with I wish to say nothing, 17.19). Learning without tiring — the outward direction. Teaching without weariness — the same line as in teaching, no categories and anyone from a bundle of dried meat upward.

And the closing question deflates all three at once.

7.3

"That virtue goes uncultivated; that learning goes undiscussed; that hearing what is right I cannot move to it; that having what is not good I cannot correct it — these are my worries."

子曰：「德之不修，学之不讲，闻义不能徙，不善不能改，是吾忧也。」

Anchor (core). He states what he is afraid of, in his own voice.

All four worries are about failing to keep moving, not about falling short of a standard. Not cultivating; not discussing; hearing what is right and being unable to shift to it; having a fault and being unable to correct it.

Which is worth noticing: the anxiety of a man building a doctrine is that the doctrine is wrong. His is that he will stop revising.

7.4

In his leisure hours, the Master was composed and at ease.

子之燕居，申申如也，夭夭如也。

Anchor (probable). A high-quality observation — students recording their teacher off duty.

Two "as if" approximations for a bearing that cannot be stated exactly (compare the whole of Book 10). This is what was seen, not what was taught.

7.5

"How I have declined! It has been long since I dreamed of the Duke of Zhou."

子曰：「甚矣吾衰也！久矣吾不复梦见周公！」

Anchor (core). An old man's lament, on the record.

The Duke of Zhou stands for the project — the making of rites and music — and to dream of him was the vigour of a life set on carrying it out. Its absence is what he registers.

With it is fate (6.10) and the phoenix does not come (9.9): the ageing and the discouragement are preserved, unedited.

7.6

"Set your heart on the way; hold to virtue; lean on ren; move at ease among the arts."

子曰：「志于道，据于德，依于仁，游于艺。」

Anchor (core). Four dimensions of cultivation — an ordered structure rather than a list.

Direction (the way), footing (virtue), substance (ren — the person as end), and ease (the arts).

The six arts — ritual, music, archery, charioteering, writing, calculation — are on this reading the outward carriers of ren. 3.7 shows what that means in practice: archery is not marksmanship; it is a vehicle for ritual, and the value is in even his contending is that of a noble person.

游 — to move at ease — is the operative word. Not mastery of the arts; ease within them.

7.7

"From anyone who has brought so much as a bundle of dried meat, I have never withheld instruction."

子曰：「自行束脩以上，吾未尝无诲焉。」

Anchor (core). The anchor for teaching without categories (with 15.39).

A bundle of dried meat is the smallest possible token. The threshold is set as low as a threshold can be set: no question of birth, of wealth, of standing — only the minimum sign of coming in earnest.

And this runs against the interest of the school that preserved it. A tradition raising its founder's standing raises the barrier to entry. This one has him opening the door as wide as it goes.

7.8

"I do not open the way for those who are not struggling to understand; I do not supply the words for those who are not struggling to speak. If I raise one corner and they do not come back with the other three, I do not go on."

子曰：「不愤不启，不悱不发，举一隅不以三隅反，则不复也。」

Anchor (core). Three of this reading's criteria, all in one sentence.

Test before teaching: the struggling is the temperature; below it, nothing is delivered.

Leave a remainder: one corner is raised, and three are left for them.

And the third clause is the hardest and the most often misread. I do not go on is not a punishment for slow students. It is the recognition that the return has to come from them — if the other three corners are supplied as well, there is nothing left that could be theirs.

7.9

When the Master ate beside a mourner, he never ate his fill. On a day when he had wept, he did not sing.

子食于有丧者之侧，未尝饱也。子于是日哭，则不歌。

Anchor (probable). The observing layer, recording that the feeling was real.

Grief that does not switch off when the ceremony ends. He does not eat his fill beside a mourner; having wept, he does not sing that day. Not because a rule forbids it — because the feeling carries on of itself.

Which is 3.4 in the flesh: in mourning, rather grief than formal correctness.

7.10

The Master said to Yan Yuan: "When employed, to act; when set aside, to withdraw — only you and I have this." Zilu said: "If you had charge of the three armies, whom would you take with you?" The Master said: "One who would fight a tiger bare-handed or wade a river, and die without regret — I would not take him. It must be one who approaches an undertaking with caution, who likes to plan, and who succeeds."

子谓颜渊曰：「用之则行，舍之则藏，惟我与尔有是夫！」子路曰：「子行三军，则谁与？」子曰：「暴虎冯河，死而无悔者，吾不与也。必也临事而惧，好谋而成者也。」

Anchor (core). Two people, one praised and one needled, each precisely.

To Yan Hui: employed, act; set aside, withdraw. Position does not depend on being used (with the noble person may certainly find himself in want, 15.1, and joy lies therein, 7.15).

Zilu, hearing his friend praised, competes — if you led an army, whom would you take? — assuming courage would be picked.

And the needle goes in. Not the man who fights tigers bare-handed. The one who approaches with caution, likes to plan, and gets it done. Courage is not what qualifies him; it is what he must be careful of.

7.11

"If wealth were something to be sought, I would do it even as a whip-holder. Since it is not to be sought, I follow what I love."

子曰：「富而可求也，虽执鞭之士，吾亦为之。如不可求，从吾所好。」

Anchor (core).

This reading takes the chapter as being about whether a ruler is worth serving, not about personal wealth. 富 read causatively — to make prosperous: if serving this ruler could make the state and its people prosper, I would take the humblest post under him. If it cannot, I follow what I love.

The office is not refused out of principle, and it is not sought for its own sake. What decides is whether anything can be done for people from it.

7.12

The things the Master was cautious about: fasting, war, illness.

子之所慎：齐，战，疾。

Anchor (probable). Observation.

Three things where the consequences cannot be taken back: approach to the spirits, the survival of a state, the survival of a person. Caution is the response to irreversibility (with approaches an undertaking with caution, 7.10).

7.13

When the Master was in Qi he heard the Shao, and for three months did not notice the taste of meat. He said: "I never imagined that music could reach such a point."

子在齐闻《韶》，三月不知肉味。曰：「不图为乐之至于斯也！」

Anchor (core). First-person corroboration of 3.25.

Not a verdict on the Shao — a report of being absorbed by it. 3.25 says the Shao is perfect in beauty and goodness; this is what that judgment felt like from inside.

7.14

Ran You said: "Is our Master supporting the ruler of Wei?" Zigong said: "I will ask him." He went in and said: "What sort of men were Bo Yi and Shu Qi?" "Ancient worthies." "Did they feel resentment?" "They sought ren and got ren — what resentment would there be?" Zigong came out and said: "Our Master is not supporting him."

冉有曰：「夫子为卫君乎？」子贡曰：「诺，吾将问之。」入，曰：「伯夷、叔齐何人也？」……出，曰：「夫子不为也。」

Anchor (core). An intricate two-layer sounding.

The background is a father and son fighting over a throne. Duke Ling of Wei had exiled his heir Kuaikui; on the duke's death the grandson Zhe took the throne; when Jin sent Kuaikui back, Zhe refused to yield to his own father.

Ran You's question is too direct. Zigong does not repeat it. He asks instead about two brothers who each declined a throne in the other's favour — and gets: they sought ren and got ren; what resentment?

And Zigong walks out with his answer. The teacher never said no. He was asked about two men who gave a throne away, and answered warmly. The inference was the student's to make — and he made it.

Which is the method working through a third party: the answer was not supplied.

7.15

"Coarse rice to eat, water to drink, my bent arm for a pillow — joy lies therein. Wealth and rank gained unrightly are to me as drifting clouds."

子曰：「饭疏食，饮水，曲肱而枕之，乐亦在其中矣。不义而富且贵，于我如浮云。」

Anchor (core).

"Lies therein" again (with 2.18, 13.18): joy is a by-product of how one is living, not something the conditions supply.

And the second clause sets the boundary: it is not wealth that is refused, it is wealth got unrightly — which, being outside yi, is not even an object of desire. Drifting clouds: present, and nothing to do with him.

7.16

"Give me a few more years — at fifty, to study the Changes — and I might be free of great faults."

子曰：「加我数年，五十以学《易》，可以无大过矣。」

Anchor (probable). First person, and an old man still wanting to learn.

Note the calibration: free of great faults — not of faults. The claim is held down (with I have not seen, 4.6; do not shrink from correcting, 1.8).

And the purpose of study is stated as the reduction of error (with 6.3, did not repeat an error).

7.17

The Master used the standard pronunciation for the Odes, the Documents, and the performance of ritual.

子所雅言，《诗》、《书》、执礼，皆雅言也。

Anchor (weak). An observation, accurately made — and there is no structure in it. A fact about practice.

7.18

The Duke of She asked Zilu about Confucius, and Zilu did not answer. The Master said: "Why did you not say: he is the sort of man who, in his eagerness, forgets to eat; who in his gladness forgets his cares; who does not notice old age coming on."

叶公问孔子于子路，子路不对。子曰：「女奚不曰：其为人也，发愤忘食，乐以忘忧，不知老之将至云尔。」

Anchor (core). The self-portrait, and the anchor for describing a person by process.

Asked to say who he is, he supplies three participles and no achievements. Driven, glad, unaware of age arriving. Nothing completed, nothing held.

A man with a doctrine describes his doctrine. A man with a position describes his standing. This one describes what he is in the middle of.

7.19

"I am not one who was born knowing. I love what is old, and I am quick to seek it."

子曰：「我非生而知之者，好古，敏以求之者也。」

Anchor (core). The anchor of de-sanctification (with 7.20, 7.33, 9.6).

He files himself under learned it and disclaims born knowing.

And note precisely what this does and does not do to 16.9 (those born knowing are highest). It does not contradict it — a man may decline to place himself in a category without denying the category. What it does is remove himself from the top of the ladder, and it is the removal that this reading rests on. (An earlier version of this work argued the stronger point and was wrong; see Part IV.)

7.20

The Master did not speak of: prodigies, feats of strength, disorder, spirits.

子不语：怪，力，乱，神。

Anchor (core). A control case — testimony to his silence.

Four things not discussed — the marvellous, the forceful, the seditious, the numinous. Different reasons, one shape: a boundary kept by not speaking.

With 5.13 (his words on human nature and the way of Heaven cannot be heard): any passage that lays out a system on these subjects has to get past both.

7.21

"Walking with two others, I am sure of a teacher among them. I pick out what is good in them and follow it, and what is not good, and correct it in myself."

子曰：「三人行，必有我师焉。择其善者而从之，其不善者而改之。」

Anchor (core).

Both directions come home. The good is taken up; the bad is turned into one's own correction, not into a verdict on the other man.

And "I am sure of a teacher among them" installs no ceiling: there is no point at which there is nothing left to learn (with you have drawn a line, 6.12).

7.22

"Heaven has given me this virtue. What can Huan Tui do to me?"

子曰：「天生德于予，桓魋其如予何？」

Anchor (probable) — with a reservation recorded.

Said when a minister of Song was trying to kill him. In danger, he states what he stands on (with is not the culture here in me?, 9.5).

The reservation: Heaven has given me this virtue comes close to claiming a mandate, which sits awkwardly beside "I am not one who was born knowing" (7.19) and the dismantling of "Heaven's endowment" at 9.6. Recorded, not resolved.

7.23

"Do you take me to be keeping something from you? I keep nothing from you. There is nothing I do that I do not share with you — that is who I am."

子曰：「二三子以我为隐乎？吾无隐乎尔。吾无行而不与二三子者，是丘也。」

Anchor (core). The explicit denial of an inner doctrine.

He states in public that there is no esoteric teaching — and stakes his identity on it: that is who I am.

With 16.13, where a student privately asks the teacher's son whether he received special instruction, and is told he did not — and with 15.3, where the "one thread" is left unexplained. Three independent passages, and none of them turns up a hidden curriculum.

7.24

The Master taught four things: culture, conduct, doing one's utmost, good faith.

子以四教：文，行，忠，信。

Anchor. A student's summary of what was taught.

What is worth noting is where 忠 and 信 are placed: they appear here as virtues of the teaching situation — do your utmost, be honest — not as free-floating universal laws. They are two of four items in a pedagogical arrangement, the inward pair matched to the outward pair.

7.25

"A sage I cannot hope to see. If I could see a noble person, that would do." … "A good man I cannot hope to see. If I could see one with constancy, that would do. To have nothing and pretend to have; to be empty and pretend to be full; to be in straits and pretend to be at ease — it is hard to have constancy."

子曰：「圣人，吾不得而见之矣；得见君子者，斯可矣。」……「亡而为有，虚而为盈，约而为泰，难乎有恒矣。」

Anchor (probable). Two steps down to what is real, and a diagnosis of the counterfeit.

Sage → noble person. Good man → one with constancy. Each step calibrated with that would do (with 5.27, 6.29 — the same empirical disappointment).

And the three counterfeits are all the same act: having nothing and displaying having, being empty and displaying fullness, being in straits and displaying ease. Pretending to a standing one does not hold — which is why constancy is hard: it requires being where you actually are.

7.26

The Master fished with a line but not a net; he shot with a corded arrow, but not at roosting birds.

子钓而不纲，弋不射宿。

Anchor (core). A behavioural image, left entirely unexplained.

Two acts, no commentary. Take with a line, not a net. Do not shoot the bird that has come home to roost.

Taking with a limit; not clearing the ground. The extension of treating a person as an end out past people — not exhausting a pond because one is hungry, not taking what has no chance because one can.

7.27

"There are perhaps those who invent without knowing. I do not do that. I hear much, choose the good in it, and follow it; I see much, and keep it in mind. That is the lower grade of knowing."

子曰：「盖有不知而作之者，我无是也。多闻，择其善者而从之；多见而识之；知之次也。」

Anchor (core). A statement of method, continuing 7.1.

I do not do that answers exactly to the 不作 of "transmitting and not composing." What he refuses is inventing without grounds.

And the last clause is a self-placement: hearing much, seeing much, choosing — this is the lower grade of knowing. Not the born-knowing; the found-out kind. He puts himself in it.

7.28

The people of Hu village were hard to talk with. A boy from there was received, and the disciples were perplexed. The Master said: "I approve his coming forward; I do not approve what he does after. Why be so severe? When a man cleans himself up to come forward, I approve his cleaning up; I do not vouch for his past."

互乡难与言，童子见，门人惑。子曰：「与其进也，不与其退也，唯何甚？人洁己以进，与其洁也，不保其往也。」

Anchor (core). The clearest statement in the book that a person is a process.

A boy from a place with a bad name comes, and the students think their teacher should not have received him.

The answer separates three things. I approve his coming forward — the movement is what is credited. I do not vouch for his past — and no claim is being made about it. Why be so severe? — why refuse someone this far in advance.

A person is not identified with where they have been. What is received is the step being taken now.

7.29

"Is ren far away? I want ren, and ren is here."

子曰：「仁远乎哉？我欲仁，斯仁至矣。」

Anchor (core). The anchor for removing thresholds.

A question, then eight characters. Ren is not at a distance, not the reward of long cultivation, not something one qualifies for. Wanting it is the arrival.

With you have drawn a line (6.12), ren proceeds from oneself (12.1), and take what is near at hand as the analogy (6.30): four passages removing a threshold that was never there.

7.30

The Minister of Crime of Chen asked whether Duke Zhao knew ritual. Confucius said: "He knew ritual." When Confucius had withdrawn, the minister bowed to Wuma Qi and brought him forward, saying: "I have heard that the noble person is not partisan — but is he? The prince took a wife from Wu, of the same surname as himself, and called her Wu Mengzi. If the prince knew ritual, who does not?" Wuma Qi reported this. The Master said: "I am fortunate. If I have a fault, others are sure to know it."

陈司败问：「昭公知礼乎？」孔子曰：「知礼。」……子曰：「丘也幸，苟有过，人必知之。」

Anchor (core).

He is caught out — he had defended his own state's ruler over a marriage that broke the rule against marrying within one's surname, a breach then covered by giving the woman a false name.

And the response is not a defence and not an apology. I am fortunate. If I have a fault, others are sure to know it.

Being corrected is registered as good luck. This is 1.14's "go to those who have the way to be corrected," lived through — and it is the positive face of 5.27's lament that he had never met anyone who could indict himself.

7.31

When the Master was singing with others and someone sang well, he would always make him repeat it, and then join in.

子与人歌而善，必使反之，而后和之。

Anchor (probable). An observation, and a small one.

He asks for it again, and then sings along. He does not assess it, and he does not lead. Another man's good thing is given room to be complete, and then joined.

7.32

"In culture, I am perhaps like others. As for acting the part of the noble person — that I have not yet managed."

子曰：「文，莫吾犹人也。躬行君子，则吾未之有得。」

Anchor (core).

Two calibrations, both downward. In learning, about like anyone else (with 5.28, any hamlet of ten households will have someone as loyal as I am). In practice, not yet managed.

The distinction is knowing against doing, and he places himself on the near side of it.

7.33
